The phase sign-off reads a NARROWER set and never a wider one — `records.ts:summaryBlockingFindings` (its state gate at 343) keeps only `confirmed` and `approved`, and only at a blocking cost — so a sign-off can never block on a row this rule does not call open: an `unconfirmed` suspicion holds the FILING gate (this rule) without holding the phase's lock. `disputed` is NOT in it: the row already drives nothing anywhere else in the pipeline — no fix task, no verification seat (`loop/work.ts`'s queue is `unconfirmed`-only), no Author queue row (`findingsAwaitingAuthor` wants `confirmed`), no hold on the drain, the chapter Goal or the sign-off, and the rail files it under "archived" — so counting it open here held two gates shut over a row nothing could ever move, and the flow wedged silently on it. The Editor's `not a defect` is therefore final FOR THE FLOW (D-33's own principle, D-34a's final triage); the Author keeps every overturn valve and the flow never waits on one — `routes.ts`'s unguarded approve and discard, `acceptCompletion`'s discard sweep, and `phrase/locks.ts:VOIDABLE_FINDING_STATES`, which still voids a `disputed` row because a lock retires a live claim rather than gating work. The release is therefore exact and matches what the Clean-stamp step reads with the same predicate (`phrase/reviewed.ts:openIssueGroupIds`): **a proposal becomes filable the moment every issue leaves the holding set**;
- **AN ISSUE AND A PROPOSAL NEVER SHARE A PASS.** `kind: 'issue'` is refused when THIS RUN has already filed a proposal, with the fix "this pass filed a proposal; an issue and a proposal cannot share a pass. End the pass; file the issue in your next review". The direction the refusal has to cover is the awkward one — a proposal raised first leaves the following issue unrefused by the rule above — and it is answered from the journal, because §4.10 has no `run_ref` field and `related_refs` says what a finding is ABOUT rather than who filed it (`loop/records.ts:findingsRaisedByRun`, the same run-attribution mechanism `revisionReviewFindings` uses). The other direction needs no second rule: an issue filed first is open, so the proposal that follows it is refused by the first bullet.

Issues are otherwise gated by NOTHING: an issue is filable at any time, over other issues and over proposals, because the rule may not deadlock the book by refusing the very work that would unlock it. **An all-good pass returns nothing**: a Critic with no issue to file and no new proposal to make files nothing and submits its verdict, and the brief says so in those words (`run/brief.ts:CRITIC_REVIEW_MODE`), because a reviewer that invents a finding to avoid an empty pass costs the book a Writer round — the failure §6.5 already names for a tool-less screen.

**D-33d** **Issues are in focus before anything else can be approved** (2026-11-25). D-33c closes the FRONT door; this closes the other three, because a proposal already on the record — raised before the rule, or re-costed by the Editor's own `verifyFinding` under D-34a — reaches approval anyway, and the Author's complaint was exactly that a round spent itself on the suggestion. **Every** approval path reads `loop/records.ts:openIssueFindings`, and they cannot diverge because they do not each answer the question. "An issue is open" is the SAME holding set as D-33c's bullet above (`unconfirmed`, `confirmed`, `approved`), so a `disputed` row releases the approval exactly as it releases the filing (2026-11-25) — which is what the two batch paths need, since a sweep that skipped a proposal behind a claim the Editor already called a non-defect would park it there for the rest of the book:

| path | `tools/editor.ts:approveFinding` | `loop/engine.ts`'s `loop_auto_approve` sweep | `loop/engine.ts:approveFinding` (the Author's API) | `api/routes.ts`'s approve-all |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| a `proposal` while an issue is open | **refused**, id `D-33d` | **skipped** — the row stays `confirmed` and the next sweep takes it | **refused**, the same sentence, which `routes.ts:refusal` surfaces as the 409 the Author reads | **skipped**, so one click still clears the chapter's issues |

A SKIP rather than a refusal is deliberate in the two batch paths: both loop over a set, and aborting the batch on the first proposal would strand the ISSUES the rule wants done first. `auto_approved` reports what the sweep did, and the projected Story row still lists a skipped proposal as awaiting approval — that is the report, with no new response shape.

Discarding a proposal is **not** refused, on any path: discarding is the Editor or the Author declining the change, which creates no Writer task and closes the row (D-34), so it stays available at any time. And an ISSUE row is never held by this rule, whatever else is open — the defects that unlock the proposals are precisely the work the loop must not stop taking.

The **Editor's verification runs in two modes**, and the row picks which one applies (`tools/editor.ts:verifyFinding`, `run/brief.ts:jobStatement` for `verify_findings`, the `editor-general` template's FINDINGS paragraph): an `issue` is judged on admissibility, and a claim whose whole case is an absence is `dispute`d; a `proposal` is judged **on merit** — does the change strengthen the story the Seed promises, is it worth a pass, does it contradict the installed text — and MUST NOT be disputed merely because the Seed never promised it. The Critic channels say the same thing from the other side (`run/brief.ts:CRITIC_REVIEW_MODE` and its `check_unit` critic job line, the four `critic-*` deep-review angles): an absence is never an issue, and the outlet for "this could be better" is a marked `proposal`, not an Idea.

The **marker must survive every projection** that names the fields rather than spreading the record: `readFindings`, `readChapter.open_findings`, `verifyFinding`'s original/result payloads, the fix task's detail line and the compiled fix prompt (a Writer delivering a proposal writes differently from one repairing a break), `phraseView.findings[].kind`, the Author queue (as `finding_kind`, because the queue row's own `kind` is its discriminator), the knowledge surface, and `brief.ts`'s "Findings in front of you" bracket, which prints `kind: proposal (an improvement, not a defect)` only on those rows so plain issues render exactly as before. The dashboard draws no badge and the Digest counts costs kind-blind: this is a field about who judges the claim, not a new dial.

**R3 — a finding's groups (2026-11-24).** On a grouped Story a finding against the Summary MUST carry `group_refs`, and the tool refuses one that does not (`tools/reader.ts`, refusal "R3") — as it refuses a ref naming a group the book does not have. The reason is cost, not tidiness: a complaint with no group means "the whole Overview", and the next Writer pass therefore rewrites all five (R2's whole point lost). The rule and its one exemption:

- **absent / empty `group_refs` = every group**, the legacy whole-Overview reading, and `phrase/findings.ts:findingGroupScope` spells the expansion out rather than hiding it. That reading is what keeps a pre-redesign finding meaningful on a book that has moved to groups. It is NOT available to a new finding on a grouped book: `raiseFinding` refuses a phase-① finding on the Summary that names no group (refusal `R3`, listing `key=id` for each group), so the crew cannot file a defect the planner can only route to all five. A chapter or plotline finding, and a legacy book's Summary finding, are untouched by the rule;
- **an id the Story does not have is refused, never dropped** (same `R3` guard, before the D-16 dedupe), because silently dropping the only ref would silently widen the finding to "the whole Overview" — the most expensive reading there is here. The `fix` that comes back with it BRANCHES by book (hardening, D-61): a legacy Story is told it may omit `group_refs`, a grouped Story is told to name every group the defect crosses, because on that book omission is refused one rule later and there is no unscoped Summary to omit into;
- **a repeat sighting widens the scope** rather than filing a second claim: D-16's dedupe merges `group_refs` the same way it merges `related_refs`, so a second Critic naming one more group broadens the row the loop is already working on;
- **closure is per-scope and all-or-nothing.** `findingResolution` reports `{targeted, resolved, missing, resolved_all}`, where a group "resolves" the finding only by the explicit claim on the row — a `group_revisions` row whose `finding_refs` names the finding. `closeFindingWhenGroupsResolved` flips the finding to `fixed` only when `resolved_all` AND the row is `approved` (D-33/D-34: an unapproved row must not close over the Author's gate), and it is idempotent, so a replayed install changes nothing. There is no "partly fixed" state in this ladder; the open finding with its `missing` groups is the honest answer, and those groups are what the next batch is built from;
- **a multi-group finding buys ONE pass, not one per group.** `loop/work.ts:groupWorkTargets` unions every still-owed group of the batch, so a defect naming Premise and Ending is answered by one `draft_groups` run over both, and the install stamps `finding_refs` on both new revisions.

**Open** means any of `unconfirmed`, `confirmed`, `approved` that is not yet `fixed` — the shorthand used throughout §6 and §10.

**D-33** A finding raised by a **Critic or reader** starts `unconfirmed`: it is a suspicion, and suspicions are wrong often enough to be worth checking before they cost a Writer a pass. An **Editor** verifies it against the artifact and either confirms it — which makes it eligible for a task and for the loop's progress accounting — or disputes it. A `critical` finding may open a task immediately, but it still needs the Editor's confirmation before it may _invalidate_ anything or move the Story toward `pending`. **D-33b scopes which question that confirmation asks**: admissibility for an `issue`, merit for a `proposal` — and disputing a marked `proposal` merely because the Seed never promised the change is a verification failure, not a correct D-33 call. D-33b's own two refusals (`proposal`+`critical`, `issue`+`taste`) bind the REVIEWER at raise time and never the Editor's COST TRIAGE: re-costing a row to any rung stays D-34a's call. What D-33c and D-33d add does bind the Editor — at the FILING and APPROVAL steps, on the row's kind, never on its cost — so the two rule sets sit in different places and neither cancels the other.
**D-34** **Nothing reaches the Writer without the Author's approval.** The chain is fixed: a Critic or reader **raises** (with a cost), an **Editor verifies and rewrites** the finding into something the Author can read in one pass — the claim, the evidence line, the cost it actually deserves, not the reviewer's raw prose — and the **Author approves** it, which is what creates the task. An unapproved finding generates no Writer work, however confident the reviewer sounded. This is the gate the Author asked for, and it is why the loop can run continuously without running ahead of them.
**D-35** Because the Author "will almost certainly approve" the serious ones, the Story carries an **auto-approve switch**: a per-cost dial (`critical`, `major`, `minor`, `taste`) listing which costs skip the approval wait and go straight to a task. It defaults to **on for `critical`, `major` and `minor`, off for `taste`**, and it lives beside Stop on the Story view. With it on, phase ③ runs unattended (L-35); with it off, the Author is the throttle and the loop waits for them finding by finding. Turning it off is how the Author takes the wheel without stopping the crew.
**D-35b** The dial does a second job, and this is why it is the most important control on the Story view: **it sets the drain threshold** (L-37). A cost the dial covers is a cost the loop will keep working on until none are left; a cost it does not cover is one the loop will raise, verify, and then set down in front of the Author and stop working on. So the dial answers both "what may start without asking me?" and **"how finished is finished?"** — widen it and the crew polishes further before it calls the book done; narrow it and the loop stops earlier and hands the leftovers over. One knob, one meaning: how much of this book the Author wants automated.
**D-34a** **The Editor's triage is final.** It decides what is `critical`, what is `minor`, and what is `disputed`, and there is no appeal step and no second reviewer above it. This is not an oversight: the Editor exists precisely so the Author is not swarmed by things a fresh reviewer said with confidence and meant loosely. The Author's control is upstream — the template's instructions and `selection_rules` (P12) — and downstream — approving, discarding, and completing (D-34, L-26). An Editor that files the wrong things is a template the Author edits (G-7), not a decision the Author re-makes forty times a day. The reviewer's original `title`, `detail`, and `cost` stay on the record (P11) so the call can be audited afterwards, but nothing in the UI forces that comparison and nothing in the loop waits for one.

**D-16** `fingerprint` = stable hash of (criterion, target, `kind`, normalised subject). A repeat report increments `seen_count` on the existing finding rather than creating a new one. This is what makes "no progress" detectable in a system where every reviewer is amnesiac (§6.7). The `kind` component is additive and back-compatible by construction: `issue` **is** the pre-existing three-part form, so a row raised before the field existed and a new `kind: 'issue'` report still hash identically, while a `proposal` and an `issue` with identical wording stay two rows — they are two claims, and the Editor decides them by opposite rules.

### 4.11 `goals`

`story_id` (or empty = a reusable preset), `title`, `threshold_soft` (integer N), `criteria` (see §5), `flat_rounds`, `regress_tolerance`.

**G-5** There is no token field, no minute field, and no round cap on a Goal. Nothing about a Goal can _stop_ a loop. `flat_rounds` and `regress_tolerance` are **loop-detection** parameters only: they decide when a Story that has stopped converging becomes `pending` for the Author (§6.8). A loop that is still improving never stops, however long it takes — and what does stop one is a sign-off, an approval wait, or **drained** (E-0a), none of which is a field on the Goal.

### 4.12 `templates` — the harness catalogue

`name`, `title`, `description`, `roles` (list of `{name, kind: 'writer' | 'editor' | 'critic' | 'reader' | 'researcher', prompt, model?, web?, agents?, aggregate?}`), `serves_phase`, `done_definition`, `selection_rules`, `attempts`, `delegation`, `escalation_rule`, `next_phase`, `max_concurrent`, `builtin`, `editable`.

**D-38** **A template is a crew definition, not an employee and not an agent.** It declares roles, and a role assigns an **employee kind** to a job. A role may also declare `agents: N` — N fresh agents of that employee kind running inside one run, whose outputs the role **aggregates** to a single answer (`aggregate`: `majority` | `merge` | `lead`). A single Editor template may therefore contain three editor agents reading the same three drafts and return one selection. The aggregation is **internal to the run**: the loop still receives exactly one report, one `selection_note`, one verdict set, and one permission-bearing answer (S-1, D-17e). This is how "one Editor or a panel?" is answered without a vote anywhere in the loop — the panel lives inside the harness, so the Author's taste is still applied **once per decision**, and D-34a (the Editor's call is final) stays true. What a run MUST surface when its internals disagreed: the `selection_note` or the finding's `detail` names the dissent and the split ("2 of 3 judged this `critical`; confirmed at `major`"), because a hidden disagreement inside an authoritative answer is the one thing the Author cannot tune.
**D-38a** **The Author chooses how many roles each crew has, and how many threads run.** Templates are the unit the Author assigns, and there is no fixed count of Writer, Editor, or Critic roles in the design — `max_concurrent` and `attempts` are the Author's settings, not engine constants. The one structural rule is per-unit, not per-Story: a unit with more than one `competing` revision waits for a selection (D-23). Assigning a second Writer _template_ to a Story is legal and means two voices; the loop then reports style divergence between them as an ordinary `taste` finding rather than treating it as an error — the Author asked for it. Every built-in employee holds Rank A; a Rank B employee may be added, but only as a delegation target (V-2).

**D-17a** `roles[].kind` names **which employee kind takes the job**, and selects **the brief and the permission row**, never the tool list (T-0, T-6a): a `writer` employee may write story artifacts, an `editor` may select, merge, and verify, a `critic` may propose Ideas and rate, a `reader` may report. All four see all tools. A kind with no instructions is the actual failure mode: an agent holding `editChapter` and no rule about it will use it.
**D-17b** Write permission comes **only from a template role of the Writer employee kind assigned to the Story** — no other kind may write a story artifact, however capable its tools. How many Writer roles are assigned is the Author's choice (D-38a): one is the default and the simplest case for P10's single voice, two is legal and means two voices, and `serves_phase` is how a structural Writer for ① and ② coexists with a prose Writer for ③. `editor` roles hold the selection and verification permissions and may write **only** through selection, merge, and confirmation (D-33). Duplicating the Writer role is how the Author gets more than one writing voice available.
**D-17c** `selection_rules` is the Editor's brief in the Author's own words: what it prefers, what it weighs, what it refuses, and how it merges. It is the mechanism by which P12 becomes executable — an Editor with no `selection_rules` is guessing at the Author's taste.
**D-17d** `attempts` is how many parallel revisions the loop spawns for one unit (1, 3, 5…). With no cost constraint it defaults to **more than one**, because divergence is the whole advantage of a fresh-thread architecture.
**D-17e** `delegation` names the roles this template may delegate to _inside its own run_ (`@underload/harness` subagent delegation). A single Editor run may therefore fan out to its own fresh sub-contexts to reach a conclusion before it commits to one revision. Delegation stays inside one run and one Story: the delegated sub-context is an **agent** of that role's employee kind, not a new employee or Work Item, and the delegating thread remains the one that acts and reports (V-2).
**D-17** The Author MUST be able to create a template from scratch and **duplicate an existing one**, then edit roles, prompts, `agents` and `aggregate` (D-38), `selection_rules`, `attempts`, and done-definition. Built-ins are duplicated, never edited in place.

### 4.13 `runs`

`story_id`, `work_item_ref`, `template_ref`, `role`, `session_ref`, `state` (`spawning`, `working`, `reporting`, `torn_down`, `failed`, `abandoned`), `started_at`, `ended_at`, `tokens`, `brief_hash`, **`group_refs`** (additive, Summary Phrase R4: the `phrase_groups.id` UUIDs this run is writing — copied onto the row by `run/executor.ts` from the Work Item, and the ONE reading of "who holds this group" that both the exclusion and the retry allowance use).

**R4 — the live hold is a fact about rows, not a semaphore (2026-11-24).** `phrase/exclusion.ts` derives it from the live `runs` rows whose `work_item_ref` starts with a HOLDING family (`GROUP_HOLDING_KINDS = ['draft_groups']` — a `review_groups` or deep-review run carries refs for all five groups and holds none of them). Three consequences are normative:
- **fail closed.** A live holding run with no `group_refs` (an old row, or a skipped copy step) holds EVERY group of the Story: one deferred sweep is cheaper than two writers installing over each other and an archived revision nobody reviewed.
- **disjoint batches run concurrently; an overlapping batch is SHRUNK, not cancelled.** `partitionGroupRefs` returns `{free, held}`, so `{premise, tone}` with premise already live still offers `{tone}`. Cancelling the whole batch would make the exclusion a serialiser and idle the queue while one group's writer works. A batch that is entirely held offers nothing — and no claim key is spent on an empty answer.
- **reading is not writing.** A group's two rewrite attempts (`GROUP_WRITE_ATTEMPT_CAP`) count only closed HOLDING runs whose journal position is after the write that produced that group's CURRENT text (`exclusion.ts:writeBaselineOf`), so a live run is not counted, N reading seats never spend the allowance to rewrite what they read, and the pair is a bound on re-drafting ONE text — an install moves the baseline and re-arms the pair against the new text, and a Seed reset re-baselines with it (§17.6).

**D-18** `runs` is the live crew panel's data source. A run row exists from thread spawn to teardown so the Author can watch the crew and pull the cord on a specific thread. The row is bookkeeping; the employee becomes an agent only for the thread it records.

### 4.14 `directives`

`story_id`, `text`, `state` (`queued`, `propagating`, `done`, `rejected`), `impact` (the list of plotlines/chapters/ledger entries the loop determined it must touch).

**D-19** A directive is not a chat message. It is a decision the Author addresses to the **Editor**, and the loop must propagate it. The server records the directive, resolves the artifacts it names, marks dependents `needs_recheck`, stops runs writing into a changed artifact, and reports what it touched; a dispatched `directive_work` review then runs as a fresh Editor thread and opens the tasks the direction implies with `createTask`. The sweep reads the queue and opens a fresh thread per task — this is not delegation: the Editor queues Work Items, and no nested conversation stands in for them. If no Editor review produces tasks, the deterministic fallback queues one task per resolved artifact so the direction is never dropped.

### 4.15 `revisions` — competing attempts at one unit

`story_id`, `unit_kind` (`summary` | `plotline` | `chapter`), `unit_ref`, `body`, `run_ref`, `template_ref`, `brief_hash`, `state` (`pending`, `drafted`, `competing`, `selected`, `merged_into`, `discarded`), `verdict_refs`, `selection_note`. The LEGACY phase ① sampler adds four optional fields: `origin` (`sampled` in round 1, `refined` from round 2), `round` (1-based), `prompt_hash` (the byte-stable hash of the compiled sampling prompt that produced the row, §16.5), and `issue_counts` (`{critical, high}` totals written by the selection, §16.6). A sample is filed `drafted` by the engine in the commit that closes its run; it is never `pending`. **A grouped Summary writes nothing here** — its texts are §4.19's `group_revisions` rows — so on a `'groups'` book the `unit_kind: 'summary'` slice of this collection is the pre-import audit only, which is exactly what `GET /stories/:id`'s `sampling` projection reads (§17).

**A draft is `pending` while its Writer is still working on it, and `drafted` once that Writer's run returns.** A later write in the same run extends the same `pending` row rather than filing a second, partial draft. When the run closes, the server checks the draft: a Summary must carry every prose block (§4.2 — `premise`, `promise`, `theme`, `tone`, `ending`, `world_rules`), and only then is it `drafted` for review. A Summary that closes short of a block stays `pending` and is handed back to its Writer with the missing blocks named. Critics, Readers and the Editor read `drafted` drafts and never a `pending` one; `competing` is the historical spelling of a reviewable draft and is read as `drafted`.

**D-21** Revisions of one unit are visible **side by side** to an Editor and to the Author, and to nobody else: a Writer's brief names the unit and its contract, never its sibling drafts (P2, P13).
**D-22** When an Editor selects or merges — or, in the LEGACY phase ① sampler, when the engine selects (§16.6) — the server MUST delete every non-selected revision of that unit in the same commit. Deletion is hard, not a flag — a discarded draft that lingers is context rot with a badge on it. The `selection_note` and the verdicts survive; the prose does not. **The group model does not use this collection for the Summary at all, and so never deletes a Summary text: R2 supersedes the deletion here** (§4.19 — an install archives the row it replaces, and history is read, not truncated).
**D-23** A unit with more than one `competing` revision is not eligible for the next phase. The gate is the Editor's selection, not the passage of time or a verdict count.
**D-24** The Author may always override an Editor: select a loser, restore a discarded revision from the run's own output, or write the unit by hand. The Editor's choice is the Author's choice, automated — not a verdict above the Author.
**D-25** **Backtrack is the only way a locked phase opens, and it is Author-only.** Backing up to phase N unlocks phase N and **deletes everything later phases produced** — the prose and the structure, hard, by the same rule that deletes a losing revision (D-22). The reopened phase starts from the locked artifact above it with no memory of the scrapped pass; the verdicts, findings, and `selection_note`s of that pass survive as provenance (P11) — the Ideas do not, because the lock that closed that phase already deleted them (D-36). The alternative — keeping chapters written against a plotline the Author has since rejected — is P2's context rot at book scale, and it is why the phases lock at all. One consequence worth naming: a phase that was locked has already thrown away its Ideas (D-36), so a backtrack reopens it with an **empty tray**, and the Critics may well propose again what the Author rejected last time. That is the price of a store that stays small, and the Author pays it once per backtrack rather than every run.
**D-25a** **Editing the Seed is a backtrack to phase ①, in full.** The Author's idea is the root of the whole chain (D-4), so changing it invalidates everything grown from it: the Summary, every plotline, every chapter, and every digest go the same way D-25 sends the artifacts of a scrapped phase. There is no partial version of this and no "recheck" — a new idea is a new book wearing the same title. The confirmation names that plainly, because it is the most destructive action in the product. **Spinning a sibling Story from the same idea is a manual copy, not a feature**: the Author copies the Seed text into a new Story. The product does not fork Stories, keep divergent lineages, or offer an "duplicate with history" option — one Story row, one book, and the Seed is plain text that is trivial to copy.

### 4.17 `ideas` — what Critics and the Author put forward, before anything is written

**Phase-scoped and temporary.** An Idea lives only inside the phase that raised it. Locking a phase deletes that phase's Ideas — selected, discarded, deferred alike (D-36). What persists is the artifact they produced, not the argument that produced it.

`story_id`, `phase`, `unit_ref` (empty = whole-Story), `body`, `kind` (`direction` | `possibility` | `fact` | `warning`), `author` (a Critic role or the Author), `sources` (list of `{url_or_title, claim, accessed_at}`), `fingerprint`, `seen_count`, `state` (`proposed` | `verified` | `refuted` | `unverifiable` | `selected` | `discarded` | `deferred`), `verification` (`{verdict, evidence, meets_guidelines, checked_by_run, checked_at}`), `selection_note`, `absorbed_into` (the artifact that took it, once written).

**D-31** The Editor checks every Idea against the **Author's guidelines** — the Goal criteria, the `tone` sheet, `world_rules`, and its own `selection_rules` — and records `meets_guidelines` alongside the fact-check. An Idea can be true and still unwanted; both answers are the Editor's job (D-27).

**D-26** An Idea is a **proposal, never an edit**. A Critic may not change a Summary sentence, a plotline, or a chapter; it files an Idea and the Writer is the only thing that later writes what the Author selected (P10).
**D-27** An Idea carrying `sources` MUST be verified by an **Editor before it can be selected** (C-15). A Critic's web result is a lead, not a fact: the Editor re-reads the source, or finds a second one, and records `verdict` with evidence. The Author selects among _verified_ Ideas first and sees unverified ones marked as such.
**D-28** Ideas are cheap and expected to be wrong: `attempts` on a Critic role controls how many run per unit, and a phase with zero Ideas filed is reported as a **thin crew**, not a clean one. **Within its phase, a discarded Idea is never deleted** — unlike a losing revision (D-22), because a discarded draft is a dead alternative while a discarded Idea is a _decision_. The Editor and the Author both need to remember it, or the next fresh Critic proposes it again and the loop argues the same point forever. **Across a phase boundary it is deleted** (D-36): the decision did its work, the artifact carries it, and the argument is not context the next phase needs.
**D-32** Discarded and deferred Ideas are **the memory that prevents the loop** (P14) — and they are exactly as long-lived as the loop they prevent, which is one phase. Every Critic and Editor brief carries the phase's decided Ideas — `selected`, `discarded`, `deferred`, with their `selection_note`s — so an amnesiac crew cannot re-litigate a settled question. A new Idea whose `fingerprint` matches a decided one MUST be reported to the Author as a **repeat** with the earlier decision attached, not silently re-opened; a Critic that keeps re-proposing what the Author has rejected three times is a template the Author edits (G-7).
**D-36** **Locking a phase deletes that phase's Ideas.** The lock is the moment the argument becomes an artifact, so the tray is emptied in the same commit: `selected` Ideas have already been absorbed (D-29), and `discarded`, `deferred`, `refuted`, and `unverifiable` ones are deleted outright. The phase's `verdicts`, `findings`, and `selection_note`s survive as provenance (P11) — the Author must still be able to ask "why this Summary?" — but the pile of maybes does not cross into the next phase. This is what keeps a long Story's brief from filling with decisions nobody can act on any more, and it is why the memory in D-32 is scoped to a phase rather than to a Story.
**D-29** The Author's selection of an Idea is a **recorded decision**, not a chat message (P3): `state: selected` plus `selection_note`, and the Writer's brief for the next run names the selected Ideas it must absorb.

### 4.16 `proposals`

`story_id`, `target_ref`, `target_field`, `patch`, `why`, `run_ref`, `state` (`pending`, `accepted`, `rejected`). **D-20** Every P9 propose-only edit lands here, never in the target field.
**D-20a** This collection is the **agent→Author** direction only. The Author's own edit of chapter prose is a direct write, not a proposal — there is no one to approve it (D-37). An Author edit is attributed like any other mutation (P11, `run_ref` null, `author: true`) and is undoable.

### 4.18 phase ①'s three collections — `knowledge`, `seed_briefs`, `sample_reviews`

These are APPENDED to §4, never inserted: the seventeen collections above keep their names and their order, and an old snapshot loads with these three empty (§16.2 has the full field tables).

- **`knowledge`** — the Researcher's artifact (§16.2, §16.3): `story_id`, optional `phase`, `kind`, `topic`, `claim`, optional `detail`, `importance` (`critical`/`high`/`normal`/`low`), `sources` (each `{url_or_title, claim, accessed_at}`, T-10), `fingerprint` (D-16 dedupe key), `seen_count`, `author`.
- **`seed_briefs`** — the Editor's derived projection of the Seed (§16.2): `story_id`, `seed_hash`, `logline`, `protagonist`, `want`, optional `need`, `obstacle`, `stakes`, `setting`, `world_rules[]`, `promise`, optional `tone_hints`, `constraints[]`, `open_questions[]`, `status` (`draft`/`accepted`), optional `notes`.
- **`sample_reviews`** — one screening Critic's one-turn answer about one sample (§16.2, §16.5): `story_id`, `revision_ref`, `critic`, `issues[]` (each `{severity: 'critical'|'high', criterion, location, quote, detail, fix_hint?}`), `valid`. These are **not** findings: they never enter D-33/D-34 and cannot block a Writer; they rank the round's samples and seed the deep review. On a grouped Summary the same collection also carries the `GroupReview` seats' answers (§17), and `SampledIssue` gained an optional `group_refs` so one wire shape covers both — a legacy row simply carries none.

### 4.19 The Summary Phrase collections — `phrases`, `phrase_groups`, `group_revisions`, `summary_scores` (2026-11-24, R1/R2/R6; `summary_scores` 2026-11-25, D-65)

Appended to §4 exactly as §4.18's three were: nothing above is renamed, no snapshot is migrated, and an old book simply has these collections empty until the Author asks for rows (R6). The catalog itself — which groups exist, what they are called, what each owes, and the order they compose in — is **product data in `src/shared/phrase.ts`**, not code shape (P15): a store, a loop or a route that branches on a group name is a defect (R1).

- **`phrases`** — one row per ENABLED phrase definition; this build ships exactly one, `key: 'summary'` ("Summary Phrase"). `story_id`, `key`, `title`, `description`, `order`, `content` (**the composed Overview — generated, never hand-written**), `revision` (how many group installs this phrase has had; bumped once per install, not once per group), `group_refs` (the group ids in catalog order — the list the projection and the review brief read), and the three **D-68** fields that record the Author's Reviewed-Summary choice: `reviewed_vector` (`{groupId: revision_number}` — the vector chosen), `reviewed_vector_hash` (`groupVectorHash` of it, the join key onto `summary_scores`), `reviewed_at` (when the choose committed). All three are absent until the Author chooses, and the read that uses them (`phrase/reviewed.ts:chosenSelectionOf`) re-validates every named row — each must still exist and still carry its Clean stamp — so a Seed reset or a scrap that deleted the history clears the choice instead of reporting a brand-new unreviewed text as the Author's pick (D-63, §17.17). A disabled definition is known to the catalog and deliberately NOT created, so a second phrase kind is one flag in the catalog and no loop change.
- **`phrase_groups`** — one row per writable part, holding its CURRENT text. `story_id`, `phrase_id`, `key` (`premise` | `promise` | `theme` | `tone` | `ending`), `title`, `description` (what the group owes, in the group's own words — the text the prompt assembler quotes verbatim), `order`, `content` (`''` = never written), `revision` (the latest revision's number, `0` = never written), `updated_by` (the run that installed the current text, P11). The **id is a persisted UUID**, and it is the group's ADDRESS: the revise route, a finding's `group_refs`, a run's `group_refs` and a revision's `group_id` all name it, so the R4 hold and the R3 closure are provable from the book after a restart. There is deliberately no `state` field here: latest/archived belongs to the revision row, and a second copy is a field that can disagree with it.
**R-lock (2026-11-25, §17.18)** appends four ADDITIVE fields that record the group's LOCK — `locked` (**absent or `false` means unlocked**, which is the reading of every book written before the flag existed and of a group whose lock the Author lifted), `locked_revision` (the revision the CURRENT lock fixed: the group's `revision` at the instant of locking, so an audit can tell a lock from a later text that merely carries the flag), `locked_by` (`'author' | 'editor'` — who fixed it) and `locked_at` (the lock commit's own stamp; an unlock mints no timestamp of its own). The null-versus-absent split is a store fact rather than taste: a store `update` merges through JSON and cannot delete a key, so CLEARING means writing `null` (what `unlockPhraseGroup` does, what `editSeed`'s reset does beside the text it clears, and what `loop/invalidation.ts:scrapPhasesAfter` does to a group whose `group_revisions` rows its scrap deletes — the flag names a row that went, so it goes while the section's text stays, D-69), while a row that was never locked holds no key at all. NOTHING else writes these four: no install, no import, no hand edit; and neither lock operation touches `content` or `revision`, because a lock is a flag on the row that already carries the text, never an edit dressed up as one.
- **`group_revisions`** — one row per accepted text of one group, `number` 1-based and sequential **within that group**. `story_id`, `phrase_id`, `group_id` (the UUID), `group_key` (denormalised so history survives a catalog retitle), `number`, `content` (the group's text verbatim), `state` (`latest` | `archived` | `discarded` — exactly one `latest` per group; `discarded` is D-68's terminal position for a text the Author's Reviewed-Summary choice passed over: retained, hidden from every current read, never selected again, and still walked by R3's closure join), `run_ref` (null for a legacy import's seed row), `work_item_ref`, `task_refs` (the open fix tasks this text was written to answer, frozen at dispatch so a late task cannot claim an old revision), `finding_refs` (**R3's closure anchor**), `knowledge_used`, `prompt_hash` (the compiler's byte-stable 16-char digest — §5's cache rule, P11), and the two **D-67** fields: `clean` (absent/false = never earned a clean pass; true = this text was the group's text in a cross-group Critic pass that closed with no unresolved defect on the group — a verdict about THAT pass, so it never moves when a later finding is raised or discarded) and `clean_run_ref` (the deep-review run whose pass earned it, P11's audit of the verdict). Both are additive: a book written before D-67 has neither, and "no clean revisions" is exactly what it means.
- **`summary_scores`** (D-65) — one row per **section per scoring run**: `story_id`, `phrase_id` (the Overview these rows score, as one artifact), `vector_hash` (the `group_revisions` vector the judgement describes — the re-arm key, §17.5), `group_id` (the `phrase_groups.id` UUID — the address, R1), `group_key` (denormalised for the same reason as above: the rail labels a row without a join), `score` (a whole number 0–100, enforced at parse time and **never recomputed, averaged or re-scaled anywhere**), `rationale`, `evidence` (the Reader's own words, verbatim — D-15), `run_ref` (the run that answered, P11), `work_item_ref` (`wi_summary_score_<hash>`). Rows ACCUMULATE: a new vector adds five, it does not overwrite, and the rail's `scores[]` is the history (§17.14). The collection's record id is **`section_score_<n>`**, not `summary_score_<n>`, and the reason is a store fact rather than taste: `store._resolveId` derives the nested-id prefix from the collection name, so a plural-named collection with a singular-sounding id would nest wrongly. The loop READS this collection in exactly ONE place, and reads it as EVIDENCE rather than as a gate (R-lock, §17.18): `loop/editor-lock.ts` requires a score row with a non-blank `rationale` for the section at that exact vector before it arms the Editor's lock seat, and the prompt renders the rationale — the `score` NUMBER is never compared to anything, and no threshold appears in the seat's rules or its copy. §2's P6 sentence and §17.14's grep check state the same boundary from the other side.

**R1 — the catalog is data.** Five groups, in reading order: what the story IS, what it PROMISES, what it is ABOUT, how it SOUNDS, where it LANDS. Adding, retitling or retiring a group is an edit to `shared/phrase.ts`. `world_rules` gets no row (R1), and `ensurePhraseCatalog` is idempotent — a route that creates a Story twice over a restored store adds nothing.

**R2 — independence and retention.** An install writes one new `latest` per group it actually wrote, archives the row each replaces, and gives an untouched group neither a new number nor a new row. So **Premise at revision 2 beside Promise at revision 1 is the model working**: the batch, not the Story, is the unit of the commit, and one group's work never revises another's. Nothing is deleted — the superseded text is `archived` and stays readable in the book (contrast D-22, which this supersedes for the Summary).

**R6 — the mirror, and no migration.** The install writes §4.2's five prose fields **in the same commit**, from the same texts, through an IDENTITY map (`SUMMARY_MIRROR_FIELDS`: `premise→premise`, …), so the Digest, phases ②/③, `readSummary` and every pre-redesign reader keep reading `summaries` and can never see a new group text beside a stale mirror. `world_rules` is never written by it. If the Summary row is `locked`, the group install STANDS and the mirror waits — the lock is the Author's declaration (L-7a/P5), the projection reports the difference (`review.mirror: {summary_id, in_sync, locked, fields}`), and the install does not fail. A book with `summary_mode` absent is read as `'sampling'` with these three collections empty: that is the migration, which is to say there isn't one, and `POST /stories/:id/phrases/import` is the Author's explicit, idempotent opt-in that lifts the five legacy prose fields into first revisions (the seeded rows carry `run_ref: null`).

**The composition, stated so it can be asserted exactly** (`shared/phrase.ts:composePhraseContent`): one `## <title>` heading per group with non-empty text, the text under it, entries separated by a single blank line, in `order` rank order, **no trailing newline**. An unwritten group is SKIPPED rather than rendered as an empty heading — an Overview of empty headings reads like a form, not like the thing the deep review judges. A key the catalog does not know sorts LAST instead of vanishing, so a retired group's text is still readable.

**The wire shape** (`GET /stories/:id/phrases`, `api/projections.ts:PhraseView`) is one read for the whole surface, and it never writes: `mode` (the Story's setting echoed, not derived), the phrase's `revision`, the composed `overview`, `groups[]` (`id`, `key`, `title`, `description`, `order`, `content`, `revision`, `updated_by`, `previous_content` — the revision immediately before the latest, for the pane's diff, `history[]` newest-first **with `chars`, not prose**, `owed_by_findings`, `writer_busy`), `findings[]` (with `group_refs`, `unscoped`, `targeted`/`resolved`/`missing`/`resolved_all`), `tasks[]`, `review` (`unwritten_groups`, `written_groups`, `vector`, `vector_hash`, `review_seats`, `reviews_on_current_vector`, `deep_reviews_on_current_vector`, `open_findings`, `blocking_findings`, `open_tasks`, `mirror`) and — since D-67/D-68 — `reviewed`. **U3's rule: the rail shows the latest text and the COUNT of superseded revisions; the one archived body on the read is the revision immediately before the latest, which the group pane shows as a word diff with each changed run grouped — all removed words in the run struck red together, all added words green together, unchanged words plain** — a row a reader cannot open is decoration, and the wire carries no prose for any older archived revision. Each group row also carries **`scores`** (D-65, §17.14): the Reader's judgements of THAT section, newest first, as `{ value, vector_hash, at, run_ref, rationale? }` — an empty list means nobody has scored this text yet, which is a different claim from a zero, and the pinned field names are what the dashboard's score rail renders. D-67 adds two fields to every `history[]` row (`clean`, `clean_run_ref` — P11's audit of a verdict), and D-68 excludes `state: 'discarded'` rows from `history[]` while keeping `previous_content` honest across a choose (the text a chosen revision replaced is a discarded row, and a diff that lost its "before" side on the one transition the Author cares about is a worse read than one more state in the derivation).

**The `reviewed` block (D-67/D-68, pinned by `api/phrase-routes.test.ts` and the OpenAPI schema)** is the same rows read as a decision rather than as a pipeline: `{ state: 'pending' | 'ready' | 'chosen', complete, vector, vector_hash, overview, groups[], chosen, stale, blocking_issues[], writer_busy }`, always present — a book with no groups simply reports `pending` with nothing in it, so no client branches on absence. `groups[]` is one entry per catalog group — `{ id, key, selected_revision, selected_id, clean_run_ref, latest_revision, superseded, issues_open, gap, scores[] }` — which is the pair of numbers the feature exists to show: `latest_revision: 5` beside `selected_revision: 4` IS "Ending r5 has an issue, so the reviewed Ending is r4", and `gap` says why a section has no selection (`unwritten` | `no_clean_revision`, with `issues_open` naming the third case). `chosen` is the Author's record or `null` — `{ vector, vector_hash, at, overview, scores: {groupId: PhraseGroupScoreRow[]} }` — and `blocking_issues[]` names each unresolved issue with the group KEYS it scopes to, so the rail says which defect is holding a clean verdict back instead of leaving the Author to guess why the reviewed Ending is three revisions old. `writer_busy` is `liveGroupRefs` echoed, the same read the choose route's 409 uses. **The score join is `vector_hash` and nothing else**: `reviewed.vector_hash` is the hash the current selection's scores carry, `reviewed.chosen.vector_hash` is the hash the chosen text's scores carry, and the `groups[].scores` list is the same rows again with their own hashes, so a rail never reconciles two copies of a judgement.

**The desk reads this and nothing else** (§10.2's U-18/U-19): a group's revision number belongs in its own pane, not in the rail; a disabled Revise control and the route's 409 come from the same `writer_busy` read so a button and a refusal cannot disagree; and a Summary the group rows call incomplete is never presented as sign-off-ready (`story-contract.ts:groupSignOffBlockers`), even though the HTTP lock gate stays exactly as §6.3a and §10.2/U-16 describe it.

### 4.20 The Plotlines Phrase collections — `plotline_briefs`, and what §4.19's rows gained (2026-11-26, R11)

Appended exactly as §4.18's three and §4.19's four were: nothing above is renamed, **no snapshot is migrated — `SNAPSHOT_VERSION` is still 4** — and an old book simply has this collection empty. `shared/types.ts:COLLECTION_NAMES` carries `plotline_briefs` as the LAST of its 25 names (the additive-only rule: every snapshot reader walks the registry, so a pre-Phrase-② `store.json` loads it empty rather than refused), and `shared/types.ts:RecordIdSchemes` gives it the id prefix `plotline_brief` — LAST in that map too, because the prefix walk takes the last match it saw and a name inserted earlier would steal these ids. The store gains one collection under the usual `story_id` fence (T-3). The row is a §4 row like any other and is journaled by the commit that writes it (P11).

| field           | type              | notes |
| --------------- | ----------------- | ----- |
| `story_id`      | id, required      | the book's own Story. |
| `phrase_id`     | id, required      | the `phrases` row this brief structures — a Phrase ② row, `key: 'plotlines'`. The scope is the **phrase row**, never the Story: a book whose phrase was replaced keeps the retired brief of the phrase it no longer has, and `loop/records.ts:currentPlotlineBrief` reads by `phrase_id` so that retired sheet cannot gate a fresh phrase. |
| `seed_hash`     | text, required    | `seedHashOf(phraseSeedOf(phrase, story))` — the hash of the phrase's **copied** seed, not of `stories.idea` (§3.3's rule one phrase down). It is the staleness gate: `acceptPlotlineBrief` refuses, BEFORE its three writes, a brief whose hash no longer matches the seed the phrase carries, and `currentPlotlineBrief` treats such a brief as ABSENT, which re-arms the structuring pass. |
| `chapter_count` | integer, required | the Editor's decision about how many chapters this seed resolves into. Positive, whole, exact — refused at creation otherwise, because materialisation needs an exact number. This is the field that becomes `summaries.chapter_count` (§18, D-72). |
| `chapters`      | list              | `[{order, seed}]`, 1-based `order`, in chapter order. **A note, not the source of the set**: `ensureChapterGroups` counts the brief's `chapter_count`, it does not enumerate this list, so a short or ragged list is an Editor's note rather than a broken book — and the tool that fills it DROPS a malformed entry rather than refusing the whole pass over it. An absent list means "the count only" and materialisation still runs. |
| `status`        | enum              | `draft → accepted`, the same two-position lifecycle as §3.3's `SeedBriefState`. `createPlotlineBrief` writes `draft`; acceptance is a separate domain call because acceptance is the step with consequences. A `draft` nobody accepted has created NOTHING. |
| `research_questions` | list         | §3.3's field, with its shape and NOT its meaning: Phrase ② has no research leg of its own (the Seed's pass already ran before the phrase was started), so an absent list means "none noted", never D-51's "waived", and the tool omits the key instead of storing `[]`. (`shared/types.ts`'s comment on this field still says "same semantics as `SeedBrief.research_questions`"; the tool module and §18 are the authority — listed as a comment drift, not a behaviour.) |
| `notes`         | text              | the Editor's own remarks for the lane. Optional, and omitted when blank. |

**The three writes of an acceptance, in this order** (`phrase/controllers/plotlines.ts:acceptPlotlineBrief`, all three in one commit): (1) the chapter **entries** — `ensureChapterGroups` creates `chapter_1 … chapter_N` from the count, because "all chapters are generated from the Seeds in one go" is a promise about the entry set being whole the moment it exists; (2) `summaries.chapter_count`, **the primitive only** — no prose field is touched, and the hand is the structuring pass rather than the drafting pass, which is the same authority the classic phase ② pipeline always had (D-3b unbent, §18's D-72); (3) the brief's `status`, **LAST**, so a caller that re-reads a `draft` after a partial failure sees a book whose entries were never declared instead of a book declared complete. The accept is **idempotent by skipping**: a second accept creates no groups, writes no count it did not need, and writes no status it already had; `created_group_ids` comes back empty and `chapter_count_synced` false.

**What §4.19's `phrases` row gained** is one additive field, `seed`: the phrase's OWN copy of the text it grows from, written when a `starts_on_demand` phrase is started (`phraseSeedInitOf` reads the definition's `seed_source`, so a third kind copies its own input without this call site changing), read only through `phrase/catalog.ts:phraseSeedOf`, which falls back to `stories.idea` for a row written before the field existed. `seed` is what a brief's `seed_hash` and the phrase's structuring claim key are computed from, and it is deliberately NOT re-copied when the Author edits the Story's Seed — which is the honest hole §18 records. **And where it is SHOWN, since 2026-11-26 (U-25, §10.2 / §18.13):** this copy is the text Phrase ②'s own Seed row opens — read through `phraseSeedOf`, and on the wire only as `seed_head`/`seed_chars` (D-76, §18.8) — while Phrase ①'s Seed row still shows `stories.idea`, the text this column was never written for (`phraseSeedOf`'s fallback, not a copy of it). Nothing here changes the column, the fallback or either hash: the change is which row the desk prints a Seed under, not where its bytes live.

**And what this collection is NOT:** it is not in `tools/artifacts.ts:RESOLVABLE`, so no crew tool may point a finding, task or verdict at a brief row — the brief is read through the phrase view (`brief` on `GET …/phrases/plotlines`), and the records the crew writes against are the chapter entries and §4.3's rows.

---

## 5. The Goal

### 5.1 Shape

A Goal is an ordered list of **criteria**, each:

```ts
type Criterion = {
    id: string;
    label: string; // shown to the Author, plain language
    question: string; // the exact question a reviewer answers
    checker: 'code' | 'judge'; // mechanical vs. LLM
    hard: boolean; // hard = must pass; soft = counts toward threshold
    fix_hint: string; // the smallest change that would satisfy it
};
```

A Story passes when **all hard criteria pass** and **soft passes ≥ `threshold_soft`**.

### 5.2 The built-in criteria

| #    | criterion                                                                                                                                      | checker | hard |
| ---- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------- | ---- |
| C-0  | the Story has exactly `chapter_count` plotlines and chapters — no more, no fewer                                                               | code    | yes  |
| C-1  | every chapter has a plotline                                                                                                                   | code    | yes  |
| C-2  | every plotline traces to the Summary — nothing in it contradicts premise, promise, theme, or ending                                            | judge   | yes  |
| C-3  | every plotline's `must_change` is actually a change, not a restatement                                                                         | judge   | no   |
| C-4  | plotlines are consistent with each other: no contradiction between adjacent chapters, no event requiring a setup that no earlier chapter makes | judge   | yes  |
| C-5  | every `setup` in the ledger has a `payoff`, and every `payoff` has a setup                                                                     | code    | yes  |
| C-6  | timeline is monotonic; no `fact` contradiction                                                                                                 | code    | yes  |
| C-7  | expanded prose delivers its chapter's `must_happen`                                                                                            | judge   | yes  |
| C-8  | expanded prose delivers its chapter's `must_change`                                                                                            | judge   | yes  |
| C-9  | no character acts against `arc` + `state_now` without a scene that owns the change                                                             | judge   | no   |
| C-10 | the ending pays the `promise`                                                                                                                  | judge   | yes  |
| C-11 | no two consecutive chapters do the same job (pacing)                                                                                           | judge   | no   |
| C-12 | prose quality at the level the `tone` sheet demands                                                                                            | judge   | no   |
| C-13 | the prose reads as though this Author wrote it — matches the `tone` sheet and the Editor templates' `selection_rules`                          | judge   | yes  |
| C-14 | the Summary delivers the Seed: nothing in it contradicts what the Seed states, and no commitment the Seed states explicitly goes unpaid        | judge   | yes  |
| C-15 | every Idea that reached a locked artifact was verified by an Editor and carries evidence                                                       | code    | yes  |

The criteria group into **one level per phase boundary, plus a whole-Story level**, and the loop reports them separately because they fail for different reasons:

| level                    | phase | criteria                          | question                                                                                |
| ------------------------ | ----- | --------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Seed → Summary**       | 1     | C-14, C-15                        | does the Summary deliver the idea the Author actually had?                              |
| **Summary → Plotlines**  | 2     | C-0, C-1, C-2, C-3, C-4, C-5, C-6 | do the plotlines serve the story the Author declared, in the count the Author declared? |
| **Plotlines → Chapters** | 3     | C-7, C-8, C-9                     | does each chapter deliver the contract written for it?                                  |
| **Whole Story**          | 3     | C-10, C-11, C-13                  | does the book pay its promise, hold its pace, and sound like the Author?                |

The first level is the one the Author did not name and Phase 1 requires: if the Summary quietly drifts from the Seed, every later level can pass while the book is wrong. C-12 sits outside the levels as a per-chapter quality check.

**G-1** The Author MUST be able to toggle a criterion's `hard` flag, delete one, add one, and change `threshold_soft`, per Story and per chapter.
**G-2** The loop MUST report progress as `"13 of 16 · failing: C-4, C-5, C-11"`, **per phase**, and MUST NOT show a phase's criteria as failing before that phase is reached with each failure naming its `fix_hint`. A bare percentage is a defect.
**G-3** `code` criteria MUST be evaluated by the server without a model call, and MUST be re-evaluated automatically after every commit.
**G-3a** C-13 is the criterion the whole product is measured by, and it is the one no built-in can get right: it asks whether the prose reads as the Author's own. Its evidence is the templates' `selection_rules` and the Summary's `tone` sheet, and the Author is expected to tune it until it stops failing on prose they like. A system that passes C-13 on everything is too loose; one that fails it on everything is too tight.

### 5.3 Fidelity, not quality

**G-6** No criterion may ask "is this good?" Every criterion is answerable against a stored artifact: the Summary, a plotline, the ledger, a template's `selection_rules`. A criterion whose evidence would be taste rather than citation is a build defect.
**G-7** Where a crew's judgment and the Author's stated preference conflict, **the Author's preference wins, including when it is worse.** A template that keeps filing findings the Author keeps rejecting is a template the Author edits — the loop must surface that pattern (repeated `discarded` findings against the same criterion, D-34) rather than keep arguing.
**G-8** The Goal is never "the best story". It is "consistent at every level, and written like mine".

### 5.4 The Goal is the shared target

**G-4** Every template's brief carries the same Goal. Crews do not each hold their own quality theory. The Goal is the one thing that makes many independent fresh reviewers produce a coherent result.

---

## 6. The loop

### 6.1 The scan

**L-0** The sweep dispatches **only Stories whose `status` is `ready`** (D-2a). A `pending`, `completed`, or `stopped` Story is scanned for reporting but receives no work.
**L-1** The server runs a continuous **sweep** over all Stories. Each sweep:

1. re-evaluates all `code` criteria for every Story;
2. collects open `tasks`, `needs_recheck` markers, **`unconfirmed` findings awaiting an Editor** (D-33), and **`confirmed` findings awaiting the Author's approval** (D-34) — an unverified suspicion is work, and so is an unapproved one. A `confirmed` finding on a Story whose `auto_approve` covers its cost is approved by the sweep itself, on the spot, as `approved_by: auto` (D-35);
3. checks the phase preconditions: is the current phase's Goal passed, is the Story **drained** (L-37), or are the only remaining blockers things only the Author can move? if so, mark `pending` (`phase_signoff` / `idea_selection` / `finding_approval` / `drained`) and **skip dispatch for that Story entirely** (L-29, D-29, L-36);
4. otherwise determines, per Story, the next eligible **Work Item** for the Story's current `phase` — a phase may not be entered unless every earlier phase is locked (L-33);
5. dispatches Work Items to templates that have spare `max_concurrent` capacity, across all Stories, until global concurrency is full;
6. recomputes each Story's `consistency` levels and `trend`, and moves a Story to `pending` (`stalled`) if it has stopped converging (§6.8) — **but only after checking `drained`, which is tested first and wins** (L-37a): a Story that has run out of authorised work is finished-by-standard, not stuck.

**L-2** The sweep MUST be idempotent and MUST use a **claim with lease**: a Work Item claimed by a run cannot be claimed again until the lease expires or the run reports. Two sweeps must never spawn two crews for one job.
**L-3** The sweep MUST survive a restart with no lost state, because all loop state is in the store (P1). On boot it re-adopts or abandons runs whose lease has expired.

### 6.2 The three loops

**Loop S — Summary pass** (phase `summary`). Objective: a Summary that delivers the Seed and is internally logical (C-14, C-15). Whole-Story; it runs until its Goal passes, then stops for the lock (L-29). The shipped loop is the research pipeline of §16.1: the **Editor** structures the Seed into a **Seed Brief**; the **Researcher** gathers sourced **Knowledge**; N tool-less one-turn **Writer** samples expand the brief into competing Summary drafts; M tool-less **Critics** screen each sample; the **engine** selects the least-issue sample deterministically and truncates the rest; the selected revision then gets the tool-using **Critic** deep review, the **Editor's** finding verification and the Story's auto-approve dial, whose approved findings queue the fix tasks that arm the next sampling round. No Editor chooses a phase-① draft any more (D-3d, §16.6), and no Reader runs a phase-① **review**. (The one exception is D-65's display-only scoring pass on a grouped book, §17.14: a Reader reads the installed Overview and answers five numbers that no gate, criterion or counter ever reads. It judges nothing and owes nothing — an in-flight run counts only as an open thread, never as owed work.)

**Loop A — Plotline pass** (phase `plotlines`). Objective: every Plotline aligned with the locked Summary and with each other, and exactly `chapter_count` of them (C-0…C-6). Whole-Story, cross-chapter. The **Writer** produces the plotlines by sampling the Summary across `chapter_count` units; Critics propose plotline changes; readers judge; an **Editor** enforces consistency and chooses between competing plotline drafts; the Author approves.

**Loop B — Chapter pass** (phase `chapters`). Objective: every chapter's prose delivers the plotline bound to it and violates nothing global (C-7…C-9, and C-10/C-11/C-13 at whole-Story level). All chapters in flight at once (L-8), running constantly. This is the **most automated phase in the product**: the Author may watch, stop, or steer, and none of those is required for it to finish (L-35).

The shape of a chapter pass, every sweep:

```
Critic / reader → "this diverges from the plotline", "this reference doesn't hold up",
                   "this spends the mystery too early"        → finding (unconfirmed)
        ↓
Editor → verify against the artifact: confirm or dispute      → finding (open | disputed)
        ↓  confirmed
Writer → fixes it (or an Editor selects between competing drafts)
        ↓
server → commit, propagate to dependent chapters (L-8a), rebuild digest
        ↓
next sweep — the Author watched, or didn't
```

**L-35** Phase ③ is the automated phase, and **the auto-approve switch is what makes it so** (D-35). With `critical` and `major` auto-approved, the chain Critic → Editor → task → Writer runs with the Author watching but touching nothing, and the phase completes on its own. With the switch off, the loop **does** wait on the Author finding by finding — that is the point of turning it off, and it is a throttle, not a stall (D-34). Either way the phase never waits on a _sign-off_: phase ③ ends when it **drains** (L-37), and the Story ends when the Author accepts it (L-26). The Author may stop or steer at any moment and is always honoured (L-10, P8).

**L-4a** Each loop MUST stop at its own sign-off (L-29). Loop S does not "keep improving the Summary" after the Goal passes: it presents, waits, and hands the phase to the Author.

**L-4** The three loops MUST have separate templates and separate done-definitions.
**L-5** Loop S and Loop A MUST NOT run while any chapter is mid-`in_pass`, and vice versa, unless the Author forces it (and neither may run at all while an earlier phase is unlocked — L-33). Two loops editing the same artifact concurrently is a defect.

### 6.2a Divergence and selection

The loop does not produce one draft and then critique it. It produces **several, independently, and then chooses** — this is the shape the architecture exists to enable.

```
Writer run ×N (fresh threads, same brief, same contract, no sight of each other)
        ↓  N competing revisions (§4.15)
Reader runs ×M (fresh threads, read-only) → verdicts + findings per revision
        ↓
Editor run (fresh; may delegate to its own sub-contexts, D-17e)
        ↓  applies the Author's `selection_rules`
select one · merge several · or reject all and re-brief the Writer
        ↓
server deletes every non-selected revision in the same commit (D-22)
        ↓
next sweep
```

**L-16** The N Writer runs for one unit MUST be spawned together and MUST NOT see each other's output (P2). Divergence that knows it is being compared diverges differently.
**L-17** An Editor's decision MUST record a `selection_note` naming the rule it applied and what it preferred in each draft, so the Author can see the taste at work and edit the template when the taste is wrong (P12, G-7).
**L-18** "Reject all and re-brief the Writer" is a first-class outcome, not a failure. An Editor that cannot find a winner is reporting that the brief or the contract is wrong, and that is a finding worth filing.
**L-19** Merging is a Writer operation: an Editor selects the parts and **the Writer writes the merge**, because the Writer owns the prose (P10). An Editor may merge structure; it may not compose sentences.
**L-20** A unit with `attempts > 1` is not eligible for the next phase until an Editor has selected (D-23). The loop may not resolve divergence by picking the first one that passes.

§6.2a is phases ② and ③'s shape, and phase ① is outside it in BOTH of its readings. On a **grouped** Summary (§4.19, §17) there is no competing set at all: one Writer pass writes a batch of groups, its accepted answer installs immediately, and the only verbs are install and archive — `select` and `merge` do not exist for the Summary unit and the routes say so (R5). On a **legacy** `'sampling'` Summary the drafts are samples the engine persists and the engine selects deterministically, and no Editor run chooses between them (§16.1, §16.6). The paragraph above therefore never applies to phase ①.

### 6.3 The gates between phases

**L-6a** Phase 2 is not complete until the Writer has produced exactly `chapter_count` plotlines (C-0). The Writer does not choose the count, and no reader may file a finding that implies a different one — the count is Author truth (D-3b).
**L-6** Each phase transition requires an **explicit Author sign-off**, not merely "the loop reported pass": `summary → plotlines` needs the Summary locked, `plotlines → chapters` needs the plotlines locked. Agents may reach 16/16 and still be waiting on the Author. This is deliberate: expanding an 80%-agreed outline makes every downstream chapter loop burn rounds rediscovering an ambiguity the Author could have settled in one sentence.
**L-7** The Author signs Phase 2 by locking the plotlines: `stories.plotlines_locked` becomes true and every `plotlines[].contract_locked` mirrors it in the same commit. One action on the Dashboard.
**L-7a** The Author signs Phase 1 by locking the Summary: `stories.summary_locked` becomes true. The Author cannot lock a phase while Ideas are still `proposed` — each must be `selected`, `discarded`, or `deferred` first (D-29), because a locked artifact with unjudged alternatives still in the tray is a decision the machine made by silence. Locking then deletes the tray (D-36).

### 6.3a Locks, sign-off, and backtracking

This is the rhythm the Author described, and it is what stops the loop from running endlessly: **work runs to a signature, not until it gives up.**

```
phase N:  crew works (divergence → verdicts → selection → Ideas verified → Author picks Ideas)
              (phase ①: structure → research → sample → screen → engine select → deep review → refine, §16.1)
             ↓  phase Goal passes
          status: pending · pending_kind: phase_signoff      ← the loop STOPS here
             ↓
          Author reads, edits by hand if they like, selects the Ideas they want
             ↓  LOCK
          phase N+1 opens; phase N's artifact is read-only for every agent
             ↓
          ... later, mid phase 3 ...
             ↓
          Author backtracks to phase 2 → every chapter is scrapped (D-25), phase 2 is unlocked and fresh
```

**L-29** When a phase's Goal passes, the loop MUST set `status: pending` with `pending_kind: phase_signoff` and stop dispatching that Story. A phase that keeps improving its artifact past the passing bar is a defect: the artifact is good enough by the Author's own criteria, and only the Author can say what "better" means here (P6).
**L-30** A locked artifact MUST be read-only for every agent, enforced by the **server** at the call (T-1a), not by a prompt and not by hiding the tool. A Writer that tries to `editChapter` against a locked plotline is refused with the lock named.
**D-37** **The Author edits prose in the UI, and only from a paused Story.** The chapter editor is a real editor — the Author types into the book. It is enabled **only while `status: stopped`**, and the UI action that opens it is one button: **Pause & edit**, which stops dispatch, kills in-flight runs (P8), and then unlocks the prose. An edit attempted against a running Story is refused by the server naming D-37, exactly like any other permission failure (T-1a). The reason is not politeness toward the crew: a Writer's `expects` compare-and-swap (T-4) makes a lost race _detectable_, not _resolved_ — the Author would keep losing writes to a machine that retries instantly, and a paragraph half-replaced by each side is the corruption that pausing exists to prevent. Pausing is cheap here because the loop is built to be interrupted and resumed at any point with nothing in memory (L-0, L-3, P8).
**D-37a** **Resume is the hand-back, and it is a commit like any other.** Resuming re-arms the Story to `ready` (subject to the ordinary `pending` checks, L-1) and the Author's edit is processed as a committed change: the Digest is re-derived from it (D-12a), dependents are marked `needs_recheck` by the normal propagation rules (L-11a), and the edited span is marked **Author-written**, so from that point agents may only propose against it (P9, D-20) rather than overwrite it. The loop does not treat the edit as a defect and does not try to restore what it replaced; the next review simply judges the book as it now reads. A directive (D-19) is the Author's way of saying _why_, so the crew propagates the change instead of merely re-judging it.
**D-37b** **Pausing is not a mode the Story enters permanently.** Nothing about a paused Story is special on resume: there is no "author-edited" flag on the Story, no reduced trust in later runs, and no re-approval gate. The crew picks the book up where it stands, which is the same thing that happens after an edit made while the Story was already stopped for another reason (P8, U-12).
**L-31** **Backtrack is Author-only** and is the single way a lock opens (D-25). Backtracking to phase N MUST: unlock phase N, **delete every artifact of every later phase** — chapters and their revisions when backing up to `plotlines`; plotlines, chapters, digests, and per-chapter verdicts when backing up to `summary` — and reset `phase` to N with no memory of the scrapped pass. The `verdicts`, `findings`, and `selection_notes` of the scrapped work survive as provenance (P11); the **prose, the structure, and the Ideas do not** — a phase that was locked already deleted its tray (D-36), and a phase that was never locked loses it with the rest.
**L-32** Backtracking is not undo and MUST NOT be offered as "revert to an earlier draft". It is a phase decision with a confirmation naming exactly what will be deleted, because the alternative — carrying chapters forward that were written against a plotline the Author has since rejected — is the context rot of P2 at book scale.
**L-33** A phase MUST NOT be entered unless every earlier phase is locked. There is no "drafting chapters while the plotlines are still moving": the phase boundary is a hard precondition checked by the sweep, not a convention.
**D-78 — accepting the final Summary COMPLETES the Summary phrase: the leftovers stop inside the lock commit, and Phrase ② opens from the compiled text (2026-11-26, T12).** The verb has always meant "this clean Summary is the Summary" (L-7a, U-16); the act now finishes the phrase as well as signing the artifact. **(a) Nothing was added to the gate.** `POST /stories/:id/lock` still refuses on exactly two facts — the phase's undecided Ideas tray (L-7a/D-29) and a Goal that does not pass (U-16) — and it has NEVER refused on an open finding or an open task; what changed is that the desk stopped claiming it did (§10.2's U-19), and L-36 says why that bar was wrong here: "a `confirmed` finding sitting in the queue is a pending _decision_, not a blocked _Story_". **(b) The same commit sweeps the leftovers, and counts them.** `loop/engine.ts:lockPhase` → `loop/invalidation.ts:lockWrites` writes the flags and deletes the tray exactly as D-36 pins, and now also moves every finding still OPEN **on the Summary phrase** to `discarded` through `phrase/locks.ts:voidFindingsOnLock` — the same void path a group lock uses, so `fixed`/`discarded` stay terminal and `disputed` moves because D-33c counts a dispute as a live claim — and moves the queue's `open`/`claimed` jobs to `wontfix` through `loop/records.ts:wontfixTasks`, L-36's own path. **Rows survive: the tray is the only thing a lock deletes (D-36/P11)** — the Author must still be able to ask what the crew said about the book, and every row the sweep moves carries the act's own journal line under one attribution (the void path appends the per-claim sentence, so "the lock discarded it" is not the claim recorded). The scope is the PHRASE being completed, not the Story, and `phrase/findings.ts:findingGroupScope` is the one reader of a claim's groups: a claim naming a Plotlines entry — alone or beside a Summary group — keeps its Phrase ② half, because that is the text the next phrase is about to write, while an UNSCOPED claim IS the legacy "the whole Overview" meaning and goes; a book with no Summary Phrase row therefore sweeps only its unscoped claims, which are the whole Overview either way. §4.7's rows carry no phase and no phrase, so "the phrase's open tasks" is every job still in the queue at that instant — which at a phase-① sign-off is Summary work, because phase-② work is opened by this same commit's phase move — and a `blocked` row is an Author question rather than a dispatchable job (`loop/types.ts:OPEN_TASK_STATES`), so it is left where it is. This is L-25b's shape, adopted for the phase lock: "whatever is still open at that moment … is discarded by the act of completing, and the count is recorded and shown". The counts are the ONE new thing on the wire: the response is the Story row — byte-stable, so a client that ignores the new key reads exactly what it always read — plus `lock_receipt` (`phase`, `deleted_ideas`, `next_phase`, and on the summary leg `voided_findings`, `wontfixed_tasks`, `phrase_started`, `phrase_start_reason`); the two leftover counts are ABSENT on a `plotlines`/`chapters` lock, because a zero there would report a decision the Author never took. The desk's confirm line counts only what the sweep takes (`story-contract.ts:ACCEPT_SWEPT_TASK_STATES` — `open`/`claimed`, and `story-contract.ts:ACCEPT_VOIDED_FINDING_STATES` — `unconfirmed`/`confirmed`/`approved`/`disputed`, narrowed by `:groupFindingsVoidedByAccept` to the Summary's OWN claims: an unscoped legacy row, or one whose every named group belongs to the phrase being completed), so its "closes N open task(s) … as wontfix" figure equals `wontfixed_tasks` exactly and its "voids N open finding(s) on the Summary phrase" figure equals `voided_findings` for the frame the read is holding — while the Overview's review line stays a SEVERITY band over three states that never counts a `disputed` row, so it answers a different question and may honestly print a different number — and the `blocked` rows the WIRE's wider open set still carries (`open`/`claimed`/`blocked` — `story-contract.ts:OPEN_TASK_STATES`) are named in their own `leaves N blocked task(s) for you` clause; that wider number is what the Overview's review line prints as "N open tasks" — the queue, not the act. **(c) Then a second commit opens Phrase ②, at the route.** `api/routes.ts:startPlotlinesFromAcceptedSummary` runs strictly after the lock and performs D-72's start on the Author's behalf with `source: compiled`, the Seed copied from `api/projections.ts:compiledSummaryOf` — the same read the Handoff card previews — so Phrase ② is seeded with the words the Author just signed. It is idempotent (an already-started Phrase ② is left exactly as it is, its copied Seed included), SKIPPED when `plotlines_mode` is not `'groups'` (that lane has no Phrase ② row, and minting one would freeze the dial on a book that never asked — §18.3's rule is that the dial is Author-movable only while the row does not exist, D-71), skipped on the same terms when this build's catalog does not offer Phrase ② at all (R1), and REPORTED rather than thrown when there is nothing compiled to copy: the lock stays, the answer stays `200`, and `phrase_start_reason` names the one verb that finishes the job. **`lockPhase` stays the one-commit lock** — the phase machine has no reason to know what a compiled Overview is, and a start that can be refused must not be able to roll an accept back — and the manual Start verb keeps its shape, including its one-time `409`. **What did NOT move:** the loop's `phase_signoff` OFFER still withholds itself over the owed seats (§17.8), and the sweep still cannot walk into Phrase ② by itself (D-72). Pinned by `loop/lock-accept.test.ts` (the void is a state change and not a delete, the scope is the phrase, `fixed`/`discarded` are untouched, the counts ride, and the `plotlines`/`chapters` legs answer WITHOUT the two leftover keys) and `api/lock-accept-routes.test.ts` (the copied seed is `compiledSummaryOf`'s bytes, and every non-starting arm answers `200` with the lock committed).

### 6.4 Chapter sequence
